--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the results and design 14 machines for round 2.

**Analysis:**
- Best machine (6116.15) uses: Repulsion (s0) → GoToColor-blue (s1) → Exploration (s2) → Stop (s3), with Stop transitioning back on color-7 (perceiving magenta). The key insight is using C=7 (perceive color) to detect the green wall and transition to Stop.
- The green wall is color 2 (green=2 in ARGoS color coding... but wait, `--clr` uses {1,2,3,4,5,6} and `--l` uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}). Green should be color 2.
- The champion uses `--l3x0 5` (magenta?) for the stop transition - this seems odd for detecting green. Green = 2 typically.
- Score ~6116 means roughly 6116 penalty points. Need to do better with clearer green detection.

**Strategy:**
1. Copy champion exactly (elitism)
2. Exploit champion: tweak probabilities, try `--l` = 2 for green detection
3. Design clean 2-state FSMs: Explore → Stop on green perception (C=7, l=2)
4. Design 3-state FSMs with better green detection

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (4355.00) uses a clean 3-state FSM:
- **State 0**: Repulsion (keeps moving, avoids clustering) → transitions to Stop state on green detection (C=7, l=2, p=0.95) or with tiny random prob (0.003)
- **State 1**: Exploration (keeps moving) → transitions to Stop on green detection or tiny random prob
- **State 2**: Stop → tiny random prob back to state 0

Key insights:
1. Green detection (C=7, l=2) with high probability (~0.95) is the critical mechanism
2. The stop state needs to be "sticky" (very low escape prob ~0.001)
3. Moving states benefit from repulsion or exploration to keep robots active
4. 2-state versions work but score worse (~4769)

**Exploitation**: Vary repulsion gain, rwm, green-detection probability, escape probability from stop state.
**Exploration**: Try 4-state machines, add neighbour-count transitions to moving states, try GoToColor in moving phase, different color detection values.

Note: In the 2-state worst performers, the self-loop transition destination was pointing to its own state (D=0 when there's only one other state=0). I need to be careful - with 2 states, from state 0, D=0 means go to state 1, and from state 1, D=0 means go to state 0.

## Reasoning

The champion (4171.66) is a clean 3-state FSM:
- **State 0**: Repulsion (rep=4) → go to Explore (s1) with tiny prob 0.002, or go to Stop (s2) on green detection (p=0.97)
- **State 1**: Exploration (rwm=65) → go to Stop (s2) on green detection (p=0.97), or back to Repulsion (s0) with tiny prob 0.002
- **State 2**: Stop → back to Repulsion (s0) with very tiny prob 0.0005

Key improvements over round 2: higher green detection prob (0.97 vs 0.95), lower escape prob from stop (0.0005 vs 0.001), lower random transition prob (0.002 vs 0.003).

**Exploitation strategy**: Try even higher green detection (0.99), lower escape probs, vary rep gain and rwm. Also try making the stop state truly sticky (p=0.0001).

**Exploration strategy**: Try 2-state versions with these better params, 4-state with an intermediate "slowing" state, different moving behaviors.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (4171.66) and top performers are all converging on the same 3-state topology:
- **State 0**: Repulsion → detect green (C=7, l=2, p=0.97) → Stop, or tiny random → Explore
- **State 1**: Exploration → detect green → Stop, or tiny random → Repulsion  
- **State 2**: Stop → very tiny random → Repulsion

The score seems to be plateauing around 4171-4297. The variation in rep gain (3-5) and rwm (50-80) doesn't matter much. The key parameters are the green detection probability and the stop escape probability.

Key observations:
- 2-state FSMs score worse (~5316) - the two moving states help
- Starting in Repulsion (s0=5) is better than starting in Exploration (s0=0)
- 4-state FSMs with GoToColor don't help
- The stop state escape prob (0.0005) seems near-optimal

**New ideas to try:**
1. Even lower escape from stop (0.0001, 0.00001)
2. Try p=0.97 with very different random escape combos
3. Try adding a second transition from stop: detect green again to stay stopped (not possible - can't self-loop)
4. Try making stop detect green with HIGH prob to stay (but can't self-loop)
5. Try 4-state where stop has TWO escape options both tiny
6. Try GoToColor-green as one moving state (moves toward green = wall, helps detection)
7. Try different green detection probs: 0.98, 0.96

## Reasoning

The new champion (3542.48) uses **GoToColor-green** (B=8, clr=2) as the first state - this actively moves toward the green wall, making detection much more reliable! Then Repulsion keeps robots moving, and Stop is the final state.

Key insight: GoToColor-green (s0) → Repulsion (s1) → Stop (s2). The stop state escape goes back to **Repulsion (s1)** not GoToColor, which is index 1 in the 3-state machine (destination index within "other states").

Wait, let me re-read the champion carefully:
- s0=GoToColor-green, transitions: → s1 on green detection (p=0.97), → s1 on random (p=0.002)  
- s1=Repulsion, transitions: → s2(stop) on green detection (p=0.97)... wait `--n1x0 1` with 3 states and leaving state 1, others are [0,2], so D=1 means state 2. And `--n1x1 0` means state 0.
- s2=Stop, escape → s1 (D=1 from state 2 means state... others from state 2 are [0,1], D=1=state 1)

So: GoToColor moves toward green → when detected, switch to Repulsion → when Repulsion detects green, switch to Stop. Stop occasionally escapes back to Repulsion.

**Exploitation**: Vary clr0 (try other colors for GoToColor), vary rep gain, detection probs, escape prob.
**Exploration**: Try GoToColor-green directly → Stop (2-state), try 4-state with GoToColor + Exploration + Repulsion + Stop.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (2726.17) is well-established:
- **s0**: GoToColor-green → detect green (p=0.99) → Repulsion, or random (p=0.001) → Repulsion
- **s1**: Repulsion → detect green (p=0.99) → Stop, or random (p=0.001) → GoToColor
- **s2**: Stop → random (p=0.0001) → Repulsion (D=1 from state 2)

The 2-state version (2752.51) is very close, suggesting the Repulsion intermediate state helps but marginally.

Key findings:
- p=0.99 for green detection is optimal (0.999 didn't beat it)
- Escape prob from moving states: 0.001 is better than 0.0005
- Stop escape: 0.0001 is better than 0.0002 or 0.0005
- 4-state FSMs perform worse
- Stop escape destination matters: D=1 (back to Repulsion/middle state) is better

**Exploitation**: Try p=0.995, escape=0.00005 from stop, vary rep gain (4.5, 3.5), try different destination from stop (D=0=GoToColor).

**Exploration**: Try GoToColor → Stop directly (2-state with even lower escape), try adding a second green-detection transition from stop, try Attraction as middle state.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (2713.63) and the 2-state version (2713.63) are tied! The structure is very clear now:
- **GoToColor-green** as primary moving state (actively seeks green wall)
- **Repulsion** as secondary moving state (keeps robots spread and moving)
- **Stop** as terminal state with very tiny escape (0.0001)
- Green detection p=0.99, random transition p=0.001

The 2-state version (GoToColor → Stop) achieves the same score, meaning the Repulsion intermediate doesn't add much.

Key observations:
- Stop escape destination: D=0 (GoToColor) and D=1 (Repulsion) both achieve 2713.63
- Adding a second transition from stop to detect green (worst performer 4761) is BAD
- p=0.995 gives 2765, so 0.99 is better than 0.995
- The topology is converging; need to explore more aggressively

**New ideas**: 
1. Try escape prob even lower: 0.00005, 0.00001
2. Try p=0.985 for green detection
3. Try GoToColor with different intermediate (GoAwayColor from non-green?)
4. Try making random transition from moving states even lower: 0.0005
5. Try 4-state: GoToColor → Exploration → Repulsion → Stop

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (2620.51) has **p2x0=0** - a zero escape probability from the stop state! This means once robots enter stop, they NEVER leave. This is the key insight: the stop state is perfectly absorbing.

The structure is:
- **s0**: GoToColor-green → detect green (p=0.99) → Repulsion, or random (p=0.001) → Repulsion
- **s1**: Repulsion → detect green (p=0.99) → Stop, or random (p=0.001) → GoToColor  
- **s2**: Stop → escape prob = **0** (never escapes!)

This is nearly optimal. The remaining score (~2620) comes from: ~500 steps × 12 robots × some fraction not yet in stop when phase 2 ends, plus phase 1 costs from occasional random transitions.

**Final round exploitation**: 
- Try p=0.0 escape with p=0.995 or p=0.999 green detection
- Try p=0.985 green detection with p=0.0 escape
- Try reducing random transition prob below 0.001 (e.g., 0.0005, 0.0001)
- Try 2-state with p=0 escape
- Try p=0.0 escape with rep gain variations

**Exploration**: Different movement in second state, try GoAwayColor from non-green walls.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. This mission has TWO SUCCESSIVE PHASES separated by a signal, and the scoring rule is REVERSED between them. The score is MINIMISED.

PHASE 1 - BEFORE THE SIGNAL. At the start of each run the loop function draws a secret switch time uniformly between 400 and 600 simulation steps. Until that step, the score gains +1 for every robot that is STATIONARY. During phase 1 the robots must KEEP MOVING.

PHASE 2 - AFTER THE SIGNAL. Exactly at the switch time, one randomly chosen wall block of the arena turns GREEN. From that step on the rule flips: the score gains +1 for every robot that is MOVING. The robots must STOP and stay still for the rest of the run.

A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. Stopping immediately is a BAD strategy: it costs about one point per robot for each of the ~500 steps of phase 1. Moving for the whole run is equally bad, for the ~700 steps of phase 2. The machine needs at least one moving state and one stopped state, and the transition into the stopped state must be driven by PERCEIVING THE GREEN BLOCK. A transition based only on a fixed probability cannot be accurate, because the switch time is redrawn at every run. The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor conditions carry no information in this mission.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 8 walls, inner radius 0.82 m. 12 e-puck robots start scattered inside a disc of radius 0.62 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.69 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots, an inner radius of 0.91 m and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
